    bool valid() const { return fd_ >= 0; }
    int fd() const { return fd_; }

private:
    void reset() {
        if (fd_ >= 0) { socket_close(fd_); fd_ = -1; }
    }
    int fd_ = -1;
};

Socket connect_ground() {
    Socket s(socket_open());
    return s;                   // moved (or built in place), never copied
}
--- hint
A small private `reset()` that closes the descriptor if there is one, and sets it to -1, serves both the destructor and the move assignment.
--- hint
Returning a local `Socket` by value works even though copying is deleted: the compiler moves it, or builds it in place. Do not write `return std::move(s);`.
--- check test | connect_ground returns an open socket
[] { Socket s = connect_ground(); return s.valid() && g_open[s.fd()]; }()
--- check test | The socket is closed at the end of its scope
[] { int before = sockets_open(); { Socket s = connect_ground(); } return sockets_open() == before && g_errors == 0; }()
--- check test | Moving hands it over and leaves -1 behind
[] { Socket a = connect_ground(); int fd = a.fd(); Socket b = std::move(a); return !a.valid() && a.fd() == -1 && b.fd() == fd && g_open[fd]; }()
--- check test | Move assignment closes the old socket first
[] { Socket a = connect_ground(); Socket b = connect_ground(); int old = b.fd(); b = std::move(a); return !g_open[old] && b.valid() && !a.valid() && g_errors == 0; }()
--- check test | A default socket holds nothing and closes nothing
[] { { Socket s; if (s.valid()) return false; } return g_errors == 0; }()
--- check test | Move-only, with noexcept moves
!std::is_copy_constructible_v<Socket> && !std::is_copy_assignable_v<Socket> && std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<Socket> && std::is_nothrow_move_assignable_v<Socket>

+++ practice | Growth without deep copies
--- task
The starter's heap stand-in, `alloc_block` and `free_block` with their counters, lets the checks watch every block: keep it exactly as it is. It never really frees a block: it fills a freed block with -999 and remembers it, so a read after a free, or a second free, shows up.

`Frame` owns a block of samples and has a deep copy constructor and copy assignment. The telemetry task pushes hundreds of frames into a `std::vector<Frame>` without calling `reserve`, so the vector grows into bigger and bigger blocks and has to relocate every frame it already holds.

Add a move constructor and a move assignment to `Frame` so that this growth never makes a deep copy: pushing 100 temporary frames into an empty vector must call `alloc_block` exactly 100 times, once per frame. The vector will only use your move constructor when it relocates if it is sure that move cannot throw.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>

// A stand-in for the heap that the checks can watch. Do not change it.
std::map<double*, std::size_t> g_live;
int g_allocs = 0;
int g_bad_frees = 0;
double* alloc_block(std::size_t n) {
    double* p = new double[n + 1]{};
    g_live[p] = n;
    ++g_allocs;
    return p;
}
void free_block(double* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    auto it = g_live.find(p);
    if (it == g_live.end()) { ++g_bad_frees; return; }
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < it->second; ++i) p[i] = -999.0;
    g_live.erase(it);
}

class Frame {
public:
    Frame(std::size_t n, double fill) : n_(n), data_(alloc_block(n)) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n_; ++i) data_[i] = fill;
    }
    ~Frame() { free_block(data_); }
    Frame(const Frame& o) : n_(o.n_), data_(alloc_block(o.n_)) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n_; ++i) data_[i] = o.data_[i];
    }
    Frame& operator=(const Frame& o) {
        if (this == &o) return *this;
        double* fresh = alloc_block(o.n_);
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < o.n_; ++i) fresh[i] = o.data_[i];
        free_block(data_);
        data_ = fresh;
        n_ = o.n_;
        return *this;
    }
    std::size_t size() const { return n_; }
    double at(std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }
    const double* data() const { return data_; }
private:
    std::size_t n_;
    double* data_;
};
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>

// A stand-in for the heap that the checks can watch. Do not change it.
std::map<double*, std::size_t> g_live;
int g_allocs = 0;
int g_bad_frees = 0;
double* alloc_block(std::size_t n) {
    double* p = new double[n + 1]{};
    g_live[p] = n;
    ++g_allocs;
    return p;
}
void free_block(double* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    auto it = g_live.find(p);
    if (it == g_live.end()) { ++g_bad_frees; return; }
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < it->second; ++i) p[i] = -999.0;
    g_live.erase(it);
}

class Frame {
public:
    Frame(std::size_t n, double fill) : n_(n), data_(alloc_block(n)) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n_; ++i) data_[i] = fill;
    }
    ~Frame() { free_block(data_); }
    Frame(const Frame& o) : n_(o.n_), data_(alloc_block(o.n_)) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n_; ++i) data_[i] = o.data_[i];
    }
    Frame& operator=(const Frame& o) {
        if (this == &o) return *this;
        double* fresh = alloc_block(o.n_);
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < o.n_; ++i) fresh[i] = o.data_[i];
        free_block(data_);
        data_ = fresh;
        n_ = o.n_;
        return *this;
    }

    // noexcept is what lets std::vector move, rather than copy, when it grows.
    Frame(Frame&& o) noexcept : n_(o.n_), data_(o.data_) {
        o.n_ = 0;
        o.data_ = nullptr;
    }
    Frame& operator=(Frame&& o) noexcept {
        if (this != &o) {
            free_block(data_);
            data_ = o.data_;
            n_ = o.n_;
            o.data_ = nullptr;
            o.n_ = 0;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return n_; }
    double at(std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }
    const double* data() const { return data_; }
private:
    std::size_t n_;
    double* data_;
};
--- hint
A move takes the block and empties the source, so it takes no new block. Pushing a temporary `Frame(8, 1.0)` into the vector then costs one allocation, the temporary's own.
--- hint
When a vector relocates, it uses `std::move_if_noexcept`: without `noexcept` on the move constructor, it copies every frame to keep its own promise if something throws.
--- check test | 100 pushes take exactly 100 blocks
[] { int before = g_allocs; { std::vector<Frame> v; for (int i = 0; i < 100; ++i) v.push_back(Frame(8, i * 1.0)); } return g_allocs == before + 100; }()
--- check test | The frames keep their values through the growth
[] { std::vector<Frame> v; for (int i = 0; i < 40; ++i) v.push_back(Frame(3, i * 1.0)); for (int i = 0; i < 40; ++i) if (v[i].at(2) != i * 1.0) return false; return true; }()
--- check test | The first frame's block survives the growth
[] { std::vector<Frame> v; v.push_back(Frame(4, 2.0)); const double* p = v[0].data(); for (int i = 0; i < 50; ++i) v.push_back(Frame(4, 0.0)); return v[0].data() == p; }()
--- check test | Frame's move constructor is noexcept
std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<Frame>
--- check test | Nothing leaks and nothing is freed twice
[] { auto live = g_live.size(); int bad = g_bad_frees; { std::vector<Frame> v; for (int i = 0; i < 30; ++i) v.push_back(Frame(2, 1.0)); Frame f(1, 0.0); f = std::move(v[3]); } return g_live.size() == live && g_bad_frees == bad; }()

+++ practice | Moves at the edges
--- task
The starter's heap stand-in, `alloc_block` and `free_block` with their counters, lets the checks watch every block: keep it exactly as it is. It never really frees a block: it fills a freed block with -999 and remembers it, so a read after a free, or a second free, shows up.

Write a class `Trace` that owns a block from `alloc_block`, with all five special members. A trace can be empty: then it holds a null pointer and size 0. Its constructor `Trace(std::size_t n, double fill)` takes a block even for `n` equal to 0.

- The copy constructor and copy assignment copy deeply. Copying an empty trace gives an empty trace, with no block.
- The move constructor and move assignment are `noexcept`, take no block, and leave the source empty.
- `double sum() const`, `std::size_t size() const` and `const double* data() const`.

Then write `void swap_traces(Trace& a, Trace& b)` with three moves through a temporary. The edge cases the checks try: a self-move `t = std::move(t)`, moving out of a trace that was already moved from, refilling a moved-from trace by assignment, and `swap_traces(t, t)`.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>

// A stand-in for the heap that the checks can watch. Do not change it.
std::map<double*, std::size_t> g_live;
int g_allocs = 0;
int g_bad_frees = 0;
double* alloc_block(std::size_t n) {
    double* p = new double[n + 1]{};
    g_live[p] = n;
    ++g_allocs;
    return p;
}
void free_block(double* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    auto it = g_live.find(p);
    if (it == g_live.end()) { ++g_bad_frees; return; }
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < it->second; ++i) p[i] = -999.0;
    g_live.erase(it);
}

class Trace {
public:
    Trace(std::size_t n, double fill) : n_(n), data_(alloc_block(n)) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n_; ++i) data_[i] = fill;
    }
    double sum() const { return 0.0; }
    std::size_t size() const { return n_; }
    const double* data() const { return data_; }
private:
    std::size_t n_;
    double* data_;
};

void swap_traces(Trace& a, Trace& b) {}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>
#include <utility>

// A stand-in for the heap that the checks can watch. Do not change it.
std::map<double*, std::size_t> g_live;
int g_allocs = 0;
int g_bad_frees = 0;
double* alloc_block(std::size_t n) {
    double* p = new double[n + 1]{};
    g_live[p] = n;
    ++g_allocs;
    return p;
}
void free_block(double* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    auto it = g_live.find(p);
    if (it == g_live.end()) { ++g_bad_frees; return; }
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < it->second; ++i) p[i] = -999.0;
    g_live.erase(it);
}

class Trace {
public:
    Trace(std::size_t n, double fill) : n_(n), data_(alloc_block(n)) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n_; ++i) data_[i] = fill;
    }
    ~Trace() { free_block(data_); }

    Trace(const Trace& o) : n_(o.n_), data_(copy_of(o)) {}
    Trace& operator=(const Trace& o) {
        if (this == &o) return *this;
        double* fresh = copy_of(o);
        free_block(data_);
        data_ = fresh;
        n_ = o.n_;
        return *this;
    }

    Trace(Trace&& o) noexcept : n_(o.n_), data_(o.data_) {
        o.n_ = 0;
        o.data_ = nullptr;
    }
    Trace& operator=(Trace&& o) noexcept {
        if (this != &o) {               // a self-move must not free what it adopts
            free_block(data_);
            data_ = o.data_;
            n_ = o.n_;
            o.data_ = nullptr;
            o.n_ = 0;
        }
        return *this;
    }

    double sum() const {
        double s = 0.0;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n_; ++i) s += data_[i];
        return s;
    }
    std::size_t size() const { return n_; }
    const double* data() const { return data_; }

private:
    // A block with o's values, or null when o holds none (for example, after a move).
    static double* copy_of(const Trace& o) {
        if (o.data_ == nullptr) return nullptr;
        double* p = alloc_block(o.n_);
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < o.n_; ++i) p[i] = o.data_[i];
        return p;
    }
    std::size_t n_;
    double* data_;
};

void swap_traces(Trace& a, Trace& b) {
    Trace tmp = std::move(a);
    a = std::move(b);
    b = std::move(tmp);
}
--- hint
An empty trace has a null pointer. Every function must accept that: `free_block(nullptr)` is safe, the loops run zero times, and a copy of an empty trace must not call `alloc_block`.
--- hint
`swap_traces(t, t)` moves `t` into the temporary, then does `t = std::move(t)`, then moves the temporary back. With the self-move guard, `t` ends as it began.
--- check test | A self-move keeps everything
[] { Trace t(3, 2.0); Trace& r = t; t = std::move(r); return t.size() == 3 && t.sum() == 6.0 && g_bad_frees == 0; }()
--- check test | Moving from a moved-from trace gives an empty one
[] { Trace a(2, 1.0); Trace b = std::move(a); Trace c = std::move(a); return c.size() == 0 && c.data() == nullptr && b.sum() == 2.0; }()
--- check test | Moving an empty trace over a full one frees the full one
[] { auto live = g_live.size(); Trace a(2, 1.0); Trace b = std::move(a); Trace full(4, 1.0); full = std::move(a); return full.size() == 0 && full.data() == nullptr && g_live.size() == live + 1; }()
--- check test | A moved-from trace can be refilled
[] { Trace a(2, 1.0); Trace b = std::move(a); a = Trace(5, 2.0); return a.size() == 5 && a.sum() == 10.0; }()
--- check test | Copying an empty trace takes no block
[] { Trace a(2, 1.0); Trace b = std::move(a); int before = g_allocs; Trace c = a; return g_allocs == before && c.data() == nullptr; }()
--- check test | Swapping two traces, and a trace with itself
[] { Trace a(1, 5.0); Trace b(3, 1.0); int before = g_allocs; swap_traces(a, b); bool ok = a.sum() == 3.0 && b.sum() == 5.0; swap_traces(a, a); return ok && a.sum() == 3.0 && a.size() == 3 && g_allocs == before; }()
--- check test | Nothing leaks and nothing is freed twice
[] { auto live = g_live.size(); int bad = g_bad_frees; { Trace a(3, 1.0); Trace b(0, 0.0); swap_traces(a, b); Trace c = std::move(b); b = c; a = std::move(a); } return g_live.size() == live && g_bad_frees == bad; }()

+++ practice | A move that copies
--- task
`Frame` holds a tag and a vector of samples, and it is meant to be cheap to move: after `Frame b = std::move(a);`, `b` should own the very buffer `a` had, and `a` should be left with an empty tag and no samples.

The move constructor and the move assignment look right, and `Frame` has `noexcept` moves, yet every move copies the whole vector and the tag, and `a` keeps all its samples. Find the bug in the two functions and fix it.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Frame {
public:
    Frame(std::string tag, std::vector<double> samples) : tag_(std::move(tag)), samples_(std::move(samples)) {}
    Frame(Frame&& o) noexcept : tag_(o.tag_), samples_(o.samples_) {}
    Frame& operator=(Frame&& o) noexcept {
        tag_ = o.tag_;
        samples_ = o.samples_;
        return *this;
    }
    const std::string& tag() const { return tag_; }
    std::size_t size() const { return samples_.size(); }
    const double* samples_data() const { return samples_.data(); }
private:
    std::string tag_;
    std::vector<double> samples_;
};
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

class Frame {
public:
    Frame(std::string tag, std::vector<double> samples) : tag_(std::move(tag)), samples_(std::move(samples)) {}
    // Inside these functions o has a name, so it is an lvalue: move each member on explicitly.
    Frame(Frame&& o) noexcept : tag_(std::move(o.tag_)), samples_(std::move(o.samples_)) {}
    Frame& operator=(Frame&& o) noexcept {
        tag_ = std::move(o.tag_);
        samples_ = std::move(o.samples_);
        return *this;
    }
    const std::string& tag() const { return tag_; }
    std::size_t size() const { return samples_.size(); }
    const double* samples_data() const { return samples_.data(); }
private:
    std::string tag_;
    std::vector<double> samples_;
};
--- hint
`o` is declared `Frame&&`, but inside the function it has a name. What kind of expression is a name?
--- hint
A named rvalue reference is an lvalue, so `o.samples_` picks the vector's copy. To move a member onward, cast it again with `std::move(o.samples_)`.
--- check test | A move keeps the very buffer
[] { Frame a("imu", std::vector<double>(100, 1.0)); const double* p = a.samples_data(); Frame b = std::move(a); return b.samples_data() == p && b.size() == 100; }()
--- check test | The source is left empty
[] { Frame a("imu", std::vector<double>(10, 1.0)); Frame b = std::move(a); return a.size() == 0 && a.tag().empty() && b.tag() == "imu"; }()
--- check test | Move assignment keeps the buffer too
[] { Frame a("gps", std::vector<double>(50, 2.0)); Frame b("old", {1.0}); const double* p = a.samples_data(); b = std::move(a); return b.samples_data() == p && b.tag() == "gps" && a.size() == 0; }()

+++ practice | A downlink queue that never copies
--- task
Frames travel from the sensor task to the downlink queue by being moved, never copied.

First write a move-only class `Frame`, following the rule of zero: hold the samples in a `std::unique_ptr<double[]>` and the count in a `std::size_t`, and declare none of the five special members.

- `Frame()` holds nothing; `Frame(std::size_t n, double fill)` holds `n` samples, all `fill`;
- `std::size_t size() const`, `const double* data() const` (null when empty) and `double sum() const`.

Then write `DownlinkQueue`, first in, first out:

- `void push(Frame f)` takes the frame by value and moves it into the queue;
- `Frame pop()` removes the oldest frame and returns it; on an empty queue it returns an empty `Frame`;
- `std::size_t size() const` and `std::size_t total_samples() const`.

A frame's buffer must be the same buffer from the moment it is made until it is popped.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <memory>
#include <vector>

class Frame {
public:
    Frame() {}
    Frame(std::size_t n, double fill) {}
    std::size_t size() const { return 0; }
    const double* data() const { return nullptr; }
    double sum() const { return 0.0; }
};

class DownlinkQueue {
public:
    void push(Frame f) {}
    Frame pop() { return Frame(); }
    std::size_t size() const { return 0; }
    std::size_t total_samples() const { return 0; }
};
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <memory>
#include <utility>
#include <vector>

// Rule of zero: unique_ptr already knows how to move and free, and refuses to copy.
class Frame {
public:
    Frame() = default;
    Frame(std::size_t n, double fill) : n_(n), data_(std::make_unique<double[]>(n)) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n_; ++i) data_[i] = fill;
    }
    std::size_t size() const { return data_ ? n_ : 0; }   // a moved-from frame keeps n_, but owns nothing
    const double* data() const { return data_.get(); }
    double sum() const {
        double s = 0.0;
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n_; ++i) s += data_[i];
        return s;
    }
private:
    std::size_t n_ = 0;
    std::unique_ptr<double[]> data_;
};

class DownlinkQueue {
public:
    void push(Frame f) { frames_.push_back(std::move(f)); }   // f is a named object: move it on
    Frame pop() {
        if (frames_.empty()) return Frame();
        Frame out = std::move(frames_.front());
        frames_.erase(frames_.begin());
        return out;
    }
    std::size_t size() const { return frames_.size(); }
    std::size_t total_samples() const {
        std::size_t n = 0;
        for (const Frame& f : frames_) n += f.size();
        return n;
    }
private:
    std::vector<Frame> frames_;
};
--- hint
With a `std::unique_ptr` member, the compiler's copy of `Frame` is deleted and its move is `noexcept`. Writing none of the five gives you exactly a move-only type.
--- hint
Inside `push`, `f` has a name, so `frames_.push_back(f)` would try to copy it. Write `std::move(f)`. In `pop`, move the front frame into a local, erase the front, and return the local.
--- hint
Moving a `std::size_t` copies it, so a moved-from frame still has its old count while its pointer is null. Let `size()` return 0 when the pointer is null.
--- check test | First in, first out
[] { DownlinkQueue q; q.push(Frame(2, 1.0)); q.push(Frame(3, 2.0)); Frame a = q.pop(); Frame b = q.pop(); return a.sum() == 2.0 && b.sum() == 6.0 && q.size() == 0; }()
--- check test | A frame keeps its buffer from push to pop
[] { DownlinkQueue q; Frame f(5, 1.0); const double* p = f.data(); q.push(std::move(f)); q.push(Frame(1, 0.0)); Frame out = q.pop(); return out.data() == p && out.size() == 5; }()
--- check test | Popping an empty queue gives an empty frame
[] { DownlinkQueue q; Frame f = q.pop(); return f.data() == nullptr && q.size() == 0; }()
--- check test | Total samples in the queue
[] { DownlinkQueue q; q.push(Frame(4, 0.0)); q.push(Frame(6, 0.0)); q.push(Frame()); q.pop(); return q.size() == 2 && q.total_samples() == 6; }()
--- check test | Frame is move-only, with a noexcept move
!std::is_copy_constructible_v<Frame> && std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<Frame> && std::is_nothrow_move_assignable_v<Frame>
--- check source absent | Frame declares none of the five
~Frame|Frame\s*\(\s*(const\s+)?Frame\s*&

+++ question | Moves, copies and a const
--- ask
What does this program print?

```cpp
#include <cstdio>
#include <utility>

struct T {
    T() {}
    T(const T&) { std::printf("C"); }
    T(T&&) noexcept { std::printf("M"); }
};

void take(T) {}

int main() {
    T a;
    const T b;
    T c = std::move(a);
    T d = std::move(b);
    take(a);
    take(std::move(c));
    take(T{});
    std::printf("\n");
}
```
--- answer
MCCM
--- why
`std::move(a)` is an rvalue, so `c` is move-constructed: M. `std::move(b)` is a `const T&&`, which cannot bind to `T&&`, so the copy constructor takes it: C. `take(a)` passes an lvalue by value: C. `take(std::move(c))` passes an rvalue: M. `take(T{})` passes a temporary of the same type, which since C++17 builds the parameter directly, so nothing prints.

=== cpp03-l06 | Practice: What the compiler writes for you
--- for
l06-default-delete-and-generation-rules

+++ practice | A setpoint that refuses whole numbers
--- task
A throttle setpoint is a fraction from 0 to 1. Someone once wrote `Setpoint{70}` meaning 70 percent, and the engine was asked for 7000 percent. Make that impossible.

Write the class `Setpoint`:

- a default constructor, asked for with `= default`, that leaves the value at 0.0. Give the member a default member initializer so the defaulted constructor uses it;
- `explicit Setpoint(double fraction)`;
- a **deleted** constructor from `int`, so `Setpoint{70}` does not compile;
- `double value() const`.
--- starter
class Setpoint {
public:
    explicit Setpoint(double fraction) : value_(fraction) {}
    double value() const { return value_; }
private:
    double value_;
};
--- solution
class Setpoint {
public:
    Setpoint() = default;                     // uses the member's own default, 0.0
    explicit Setpoint(double fraction) : value_(fraction) {}
    Setpoint(int) = delete;                   // an exact match for an int, and forbidden
    double value() const { return value_; }
private:
    double value_ = 0.0;
};
--- hint
Declaring any constructor stops the compiler from writing the default one. `Setpoint() = default;` asks for it back.
--- hint
A deleted function still takes part in overload resolution. For an `int` argument, `Setpoint(int)` is the best match, so the compiler picks it, finds it deleted, and stops.
--- check test | A default setpoint is 0.0
Setpoint{}.value() == 0.0
--- check test | A fraction is stored
Setpoint{0.7}.value() == 0.7
--- check test | An int is refused, a double is accepted
!std::is_constructible_v<Setpoint, int> && std::is_constructible_v<Setpoint, double>
--- check test | A double does not become a Setpoint on its own
!std::is_convertible_v<double, Setpoint>
--- check source | The default constructor is defaulted
Setpoint\s*\(\s*\)\s*=\s*default

+++ practice | A destructor that logs, and moves that stay
--- task
During a debugging session someone added a destructor to `Telemetry` that counts destructions. It must stay. But `Telemetry` used to move cheaply, and now every move copies the whole sample vector, because a user-declared destructor stops the compiler from declaring the move operations.

Bring the moves back without writing a single body: declare the copy constructor, copy assignment, move constructor and move assignment, each `= default`. Afterwards a `Telemetry` must be copyable, and its moves must be `noexcept` and keep the sample buffer.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <vector>

int g_destroyed = 0;

class Telemetry {
public:
    Telemetry(std::string name, std::vector<double> samples) : name_(name), samples_(samples) {}
    ~Telemetry() { ++g_destroyed; }       // added while debugging: keep it
    const std::string& name() const { return name_; }
    std::size_t size() const { return samples_.size(); }
    const double* data() const { return samples_.data(); }
private:
    std::string name_;
    std::vector<double> samples_;
};
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <vector>

int g_destroyed = 0;

class Telemetry {
public:
    Telemetry(std::string name, std::vector<double> samples) : name_(name), samples_(samples) {}
    ~Telemetry() { ++g_destroyed; }       // added while debugging: keep it

    // The destructor suppressed the implicit moves. Declaring all four brings them back,
    // and says in one place what copying and moving mean.
    Telemetry(const Telemetry&) = default;
    Telemetry& operator=(const Telemetry&) = default;
    Telemetry(Telemetry&&) = default;
    Telemetry& operator=(Telemetry&&) = default;

    const std::string& name() const { return name_; }
    std::size_t size() const { return samples_.size(); }
    const double* data() const { return samples_.data(); }
private:
    std::string name_;
    std::vector<double> samples_;
};
--- hint
Look up the "destructor" row of the table: copy generated (deprecated), move not declared. An rvalue then goes to the copy constructor.
--- hint
A defaulted move constructor moves each member, and it is `noexcept` because `std::string` and `std::vector` have `noexcept` moves.
--- check test | Moves are noexcept
std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<Telemetry> && std::is_nothrow_move_assignable_v<Telemetry>
--- check test | A move keeps the sample buffer
[] { Telemetry a("imu", std::vector<double>(100, 1.0)); const double* p = a.data(); Telemetry b = std::move(a); return b.data() == p && b.size() == 100; }()
--- check test | Move assignment keeps it too
[] { Telemetry a("imu", std::vector<double>(10, 1.0)); Telemetry b("gps", {}); const double* p = a.data(); b = std::move(a); return b.data() == p && b.name() == "imu"; }()
--- check test | Copies are still deep
[] { Telemetry a("imu", {1.0, 2.0}); Telemetry b = a; return b.data() != a.data() && b.size() == 2 && b.name() == "imu"; }()
--- check test | The vector's growth moves its elements
[] { std::vector<Telemetry> v; v.push_back(Telemetry("first", std::vector<double>(8, 0.0))); const double* p = v[0].data(); for (int i = 0; i < 40; ++i) v.push_back(Telemetry("t", {})); return v[0].data() == p; }()
--- check test | The destructor still counts
[] { int before = g_destroyed; { Telemetry a("x", {}); Telemetry b = std::move(a); } return g_destroyed == before + 2; }()

+++ practice | An estimator you can copy
--- task
`Estimator` holds its optional model through a `std::unique_ptr<Model>`. Because a `unique_ptr` cannot be copied, the compiler defines the class's copy operations as deleted, so an `Estimator` is move-only. The ground-test code needs to copy estimators.

Make `Estimator` copyable, with **deep** copies: a copy gets a `Model` of its own. Keep its moves cheap: they must be `noexcept` and hand over the same `Model`. Take care of the edge cases:

- copying an estimator with no model gives one with no model;
- assigning an estimator with no model over one that has a model leaves no model;
- `e = e` changes nothing.
--- starter
#include <memory>

struct Model { double gain; double bias; };

class Estimator {
public:
    Estimator() = default;                                              // no model
    explicit Estimator(Model m) : model_(std::make_unique<Model>(m)) {}
    bool has_model() const { return model_ != nullptr; }
    double estimate(double x) const { return model_ ? model_->gain * x + model_->bias : x; }
    void set_gain(double g) { if (model_) model_->gain = g; }
    const Model* model_ptr() const { return model_.get(); }
private:
    std::unique_ptr<Model> model_;
};
--- solution
#include <memory>

struct Model { double gain; double bias; };

class Estimator {
public:
    Estimator() = default;                                              // no model
    explicit Estimator(Model m) : model_(std::make_unique<Model>(m)) {}

    // Deep copies: a Model of our own, or none.
    Estimator(const Estimator& o) : model_(clone(o)) {}
    Estimator& operator=(const Estimator& o) {
        if (this != &o) model_ = clone(o);
        return *this;
    }
    // Declaring the copy pair suppressed the moves: ask for them back.
    Estimator(Estimator&&) noexcept = default;
    Estimator& operator=(Estimator&&) noexcept = default;

    bool has_model() const { return model_ != nullptr; }
    double estimate(double x) const { return model_ ? model_->gain * x + model_->bias : x; }
    void set_gain(double g) { if (model_) model_->gain = g; }
    const Model* model_ptr() const { return model_.get(); }

private:
    static std::unique_ptr<Model> clone(const Estimator& o) {
        return o.model_ ? std::make_unique<Model>(*o.model_) : nullptr;
    }
    std::unique_ptr<Model> model_;
};
--- hint
A deep copy of a `unique_ptr` member is `std::make_unique<Model>(*o.model_)`, but only when `o.model_` is not null.
--- hint
Once you declare the copy pair, the compiler declares no move operations, and moves would fall back to your deep copy. Declare the move pair `= default` too.
--- check test | A copy has its own model with the same values
[] { Estimator a(Model{2.0, 1.0}); Estimator b = a; return b.has_model() && b.model_ptr() != a.model_ptr() && b.estimate(3.0) == 7.0; }()
--- check test | Changing the copy leaves the original alone
[] { Estimator a(Model{2.0, 1.0}); Estimator b = a; b.set_gain(10.0); return a.estimate(1.0) == 3.0 && b.estimate(1.0) == 11.0; }()
--- check test | Copying an estimator with no model
[] { Estimator a; Estimator b = a; return !b.has_model() && b.estimate(5.0) == 5.0; }()
--- check test | Assigning no model over a model
[] { Estimator a(Model{2.0, 0.0}); Estimator none; a = none; return !a.has_model(); }()
--- check test | Self-assignment keeps the model
[] { Estimator a(Model{3.0, 0.0}); Estimator& r = a; a = r; return a.has_model() && a.estimate(2.0) == 6.0; }()
--- check test | A move hands over the same model
[] { Estimator a(Model{1.0, 1.0}); const Model* p = a.model_ptr(); Estimator b = std::move(a); Estimator c; c = std::move(b); return c.model_ptr() == p && !a.has_model(); }()
--- check test | Copyable, with noexcept moves
std::is_copy_constructible_v<Estimator> && std::is_copy_assignable_v<Estimator> && std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<Estimator> && std::is_nothrow_move_assignable_v<Estimator>

+++ practice | Moves switched off, and everything broke
--- task
A teammate decided frames should "only ever be copied" and deleted the move operations of `Frame`. Now `make_frame` and `make_batch` no longer compile, even though a `Frame` can be copied.

Fix `Frame` so both functions compile and work. Do not change the two functions. Moving a frame must also be cheap again: its move constructor must be `noexcept`, so it does not copy the 64 samples.
--- starter
#include <vector>

class Frame {
public:
    explicit Frame(int id) : id_(id), samples_(64, 0.0) {}
    Frame(const Frame&) = default;
    Frame& operator=(const Frame&) = default;
    Frame(Frame&&) = delete;                 // "we only ever copy frames"
    Frame& operator=(Frame&&) = delete;
    int id() const { return id_; }
    std::size_t size() const { return samples_.size(); }
private:
    int id_;
    std::vector<double> samples_;
};

Frame make_frame(int id) {
    Frame f(id);
    return f;
}

std::vector<Frame> make_batch(int n) {
    std::vector<Frame> v;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) v.push_back(Frame(i));
    return v;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

// Rule of zero: every member manages itself, so declare none of the special members.
class Frame {
public:
    explicit Frame(int id) : id_(id), samples_(64, 0.0) {}
    int id() const { return id_; }
    std::size_t size() const { return samples_.size(); }
private:
    int id_;
    std::vector<double> samples_;
};

Frame make_frame(int id) {
    Frame f(id);
    return f;
}

std::vector<Frame> make_batch(int n) {
    std::vector<Frame> v;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) v.push_back(Frame(i));
    return v;
}
--- hint
A deleted function still takes part in overload resolution. For an rvalue, the deleted move constructor is the best match, so the compiler picks it and stops. It never falls back to the copy.
--- hint
Deleting the moves is not the same as not declaring them. Here every member manages itself, so the simplest fix declares none of the special members at all.
--- check test | make_frame returns the right frame
make_frame(7).id() == 7 && make_frame(7).size() == 64
--- check test | make_batch builds the frames in order
[] { std::vector<Frame> v = make_batch(5); for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) if (v[i].id() != i) return false; return v.size() == 5; }()
--- check test | Frames are still copyable
std::is_copy_constructible_v<Frame> && std::is_copy_assignable_v<Frame>
--- check test | Moving a frame is noexcept
std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<Frame> && std::is_nothrow_move_assignable_v<Frame>

+++ practice | A bank of channels that owns them
--- task
The starter has a stand-in for a payload power switch, with channels 0 to 7. `channel_on` and `channel_off` switch a channel, and the counters let the checks see what happened; `g_switch_ons` counts how many times any channel was actually switched on. Keep that code as it is.

First write `UniqueChannel`, a move-only owner of one channel:

- `UniqueChannel()` holds no channel (-1); `explicit UniqueChannel(int ch)` switches `ch` on;
- its destructor switches its channel off, if it holds one;
- the copy pair is deleted; the move constructor and move assignment are `noexcept` and leave the source holding -1. Move assignment switches off the channel the target held before;
- `int channel() const`.

Then write `ChannelBank`, which keeps its channels in a `std::vector<UniqueChannel>` and declares **none** of the five special members:

- `void add(int ch)` switches `ch` on and keeps it, unless the bank already holds it, in which case it does nothing;
- `bool holds(int ch) const`, `std::size_t size() const` and `void release_all()`, which switches every channel off.

A bank must be move-only too, and moving a bank, or its vector growing, must never switch a channel off and on again.
--- starter
#include <vector>

// A stand-in for the payload power switch. Do not change it.
int g_claimed = 0;
int g_errors = 0;
int g_switch_ons = 0;
bool g_on[8] = {};
void channel_on(int ch) { if (!g_on[ch]) { g_on[ch] = true; ++g_claimed; ++g_switch_ons; } }
void channel_off(int ch) { if (!g_on[ch]) { ++g_errors; return; } g_on[ch] = false; --g_claimed; }

class UniqueChannel {
public:
    UniqueChannel() {}
    int channel() const { return -1; }
};

class ChannelBank {
public:
    void add(int ch) {}
    bool holds(int ch) const { return false; }
    std::size_t size() const { return 0; }
    void release_all() {}
};
--- solution
#include <vector>

// A stand-in for the payload power switch. Do not change it.
int g_claimed = 0;
int g_errors = 0;
int g_switch_ons = 0;
bool g_on[8] = {};
void channel_on(int ch) { if (!g_on[ch]) { g_on[ch] = true; ++g_claimed; ++g_switch_ons; } }
void channel_off(int ch) { if (!g_on[ch]) { ++g_errors; return; } g_on[ch] = false; --g_claimed; }

// The one class that manages the resource by hand: all five declared.
class UniqueChannel {
public:
    UniqueChannel() = default;
    explicit UniqueChannel(int ch) : ch_(ch) { channel_on(ch_); }
    ~UniqueChannel() { reset(); }
    UniqueChannel(const UniqueChannel&) = delete;
    UniqueChannel& operator=(const UniqueChannel&) = delete;
    UniqueChannel(UniqueChannel&& o) noexcept : ch_(o.ch_) { o.ch_ = -1; }
    UniqueChannel& operator=(UniqueChannel&& o) noexcept {
        if (this != &o) {
            reset();
            ch_ = o.ch_;
            o.ch_ = -1;
        }
        return *this;
    }
    int channel() const { return ch_; }
private:
    void reset() {
        if (ch_ >= 0) { channel_off(ch_); ch_ = -1; }
    }
    int ch_ = -1;
};

// Rule of zero: the vector of move-only owners makes the bank move-only for free.
class ChannelBank {
public:
    void add(int ch) {
        if (!holds(ch)) channels_.push_back(UniqueChannel(ch));
    }
    bool holds(int ch) const {
        for (const UniqueChannel& c : channels_) {
            if (c.channel() == ch) return true;
        }
        return false;
    }
    std::size_t size() const { return channels_.size(); }
    void release_all() { channels_.clear(); }
private:
    std::vector<UniqueChannel> channels_;
};
--- hint
`UniqueChannel` is the only class that touches the switch by hand, so it is the only one that needs the five. A private `reset()` serves the destructor and the move assignment.
--- hint
A class whose only member is a `std::vector<UniqueChannel>` gets a `noexcept` move without declaring anything, and copying it does not compile, because the vector would have to copy a move-only element.
--- hint
`release_all()` is `channels_.clear()`: destroying the owners switches the channels off.
--- check test | Adding channels switches them on, once each
[] { int ons = g_switch_ons; { ChannelBank b; b.add(1); b.add(3); b.add(1); if (b.size() != 2 || g_claimed != 2 || !b.holds(3)) return false; } return g_claimed == 0 && g_switch_ons == ons + 2 && g_errors == 0; }()
--- check test | Growth never switches a channel off and on again
[] { int ons = g_switch_ons; { ChannelBank b; for (int ch = 0; ch < 8; ++ch) b.add(ch); if (g_claimed != 8) return false; } return g_switch_ons == ons + 8 && g_claimed == 0 && g_errors == 0; }()
--- check test | Moving a bank keeps every channel on
[] { int ons = g_switch_ons; ChannelBank a; a.add(2); a.add(5); ChannelBank b = std::move(a); return b.holds(2) && b.holds(5) && g_claimed == 2 && g_switch_ons == ons + 2; }()
--- check test | release_all switches everything off
[] { ChannelBank b; b.add(4); b.add(6); b.release_all(); return b.size() == 0 && g_claimed == 0 && g_errors == 0; }()
--- check test | Move assignment of a channel switches the old one off
[] { UniqueChannel a(1); UniqueChannel b(2); b = std::move(a); return b.channel() == 1 && a.channel() == -1 && !g_on[2] && g_on[1]; }()
--- check test | UniqueChannel is move-only, and both have noexcept moves
!std::is_copy_constructible_v<UniqueChannel> && !std::is_copy_assignable_v<UniqueChannel> && std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<UniqueChannel> && std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<ChannelBank> && std::is_nothrow_move_assignable_v<ChannelBank>
--- check source absent | ChannelBank declares none of the five

+++ question | One declaration, four lines
--- ask
This struct declares only a move assignment operator.

```cpp
struct Cfg {
    std::string name;
    Cfg& operator=(Cfg&&) = default;
};

Cfg a;                  // (1)
Cfg b = a;              // (2)
Cfg c = std::move(a);   // (3)
b = std::move(c);       // (4)
```

Which lines fail to compile?
--- choice
None: every missing member is generated.
--- choice
Only (2), because the copy constructor is deleted; (3) uses the move constructor.
--- choice correct
(2) and (3): the copy constructor is deleted, and there is no move constructor, so both reach the deleted copy.
--- choice
(1), (2) and (3): declaring any special member also removes the default constructor.
--- why
Declaring a move assignment makes the copy constructor and copy assignment deleted, and leaves the move constructor not declared. The default constructor is still generated, because no constructor was declared, so (1) compiles, and (4) uses the declared move assignment. Line (2) calls the deleted copy constructor. Line (3) has no move constructor to call, so the rvalue goes to the only candidate, the deleted copy constructor, and fails too.

=== cpp03-l07 | Practice: The rule of five, the rule of zero, and copy-and-swap
--- for
l07-rule-of-five-and-zero

+++ practice | A calibration table with nothing to write
--- task
The starter's `Calibration` keeps its table in a raw `new[]` block, so it had to write a destructor, a copy constructor and a copy assignment. Because it declares those, it has no move operations, so every move copies the table.

Rewrite it by the **rule of zero**. Keep the public interface and `interpolate` exactly as they behave now, but store the table in a `std::vector<double>` and declare none of the five special members. Afterwards copies must still be deep, and moves must be `noexcept`.

`interpolate(x)` treats entry i as the value at x = i, draws straight lines between entries, clamps x to the ends of the table, and returns 0.0 for an empty table.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>

class Calibration {
public:
    Calibration(std::size_t n, double fill) : n_(n), table_(new double[n]) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n_; ++i) table_[i] = fill;
    }
    ~Calibration() { delete[] table_; }
    Calibration(const Calibration& o) : n_(o.n_), table_(new double[o.n_]) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n_; ++i) table_[i] = o.table_[i];
    }
    Calibration& operator=(const Calibration& o) {
        if (this != &o) {
            double* t = new double[o.n_];
            for (std::size_t i = 0; i < o.n_; ++i) t[i] = o.table_[i];
            delete[] table_;
            table_ = t;
            n_ = o.n_;
        }
        return *this;
    }
    std::size_t size() const { return n_; }
    double& operator[](std::size_t i) { return table_[i]; }
    double operator[](std::size_t i) const { return table_[i]; }
    double interpolate(double x) const {
        if (n_ == 0) return 0.0;
        if (x <= 0.0) return table_[0];
        if (x >= static_cast<double>(n_ - 1)) return table_[n_ - 1];
        const std::size_t i = static_cast<std::size_t>(x);
        const double f = x - static_cast<double>(i);
        return table_[i] + f * (table_[i + 1] - table_[i]);
    }
private:
    std::size_t n_;
    double* table_;
};
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <vector>

// Rule of zero: the vector copies deeply, moves cheaply and frees itself,
// so the compiler-written special members are already right.
class Calibration {
public:
    Calibration(std::size_t n, double fill) : table_(n, fill) {}
    std::size_t size() const { return table_.size(); }
    double& operator[](std::size_t i) { return table_[i]; }
    double operator[](std::size_t i) const { return table_[i]; }
    double interpolate(double x) const {
        const std::size_t n = table_.size();
        if (n == 0) return 0.0;
        if (x <= 0.0) return table_[0];
        if (x >= static_cast<double>(n - 1)) return table_[n - 1];
        const std::size_t i = static_cast<std::size_t>(x);
        const double f = x - static_cast<double>(i);
        return table_[i] + f * (table_[i + 1] - table_[i]);
    }
private:
    std::vector<double> table_;
};
--- hint
Once the table is a `std::vector<double>`, the size is `table_.size()`, and the separate `n_` member can go.
--- hint
Delete the destructor, the copy constructor and the copy assignment. With nothing declared, the compiler generates all five, and they call the vector's own.
--- check test | Interpolation between entries
[] { Calibration c(3, 0.0); c[0] = 0.0; c[1] = 10.0; c[2] = 30.0; return c.interpolate(0.5) == 5.0 && c.interpolate(1.25) == 15.0; }()
--- check test | Clamped at both ends, and 0 for an empty table
[] { Calibration c(2, 0.0); c[0] = 1.0; c[1] = 3.0; Calibration e(0, 0.0); return c.interpolate(-4.0) == 1.0 && c.interpolate(9.0) == 3.0 && e.interpolate(1.0) == 0.0; }()
--- check test | Copies are deep
[] { Calibration a(2, 1.0); Calibration b = a; b[0] = 5.0; Calibration c(1, 0.0); c = a; c[1] = 9.0; return a[0] == 1.0 && a[1] == 1.0 && c.size() == 2; }()
--- check test | Moves are noexcept now
std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<Calibration> && std::is_nothrow_move_assignable_v<Calibration>
--- check source absent | None of the five, and no raw new or delete
~Calibration|operator\s*=|new\s+double|delete\s*\[

+++ practice | Assignment written once
--- task
The starter's heap stand-in, `alloc_block` and `free_block` with their counters, lets the checks watch every block: keep it exactly as it is. It never really frees a block: it fills a freed block with -999 and remembers it, so a read after a free, or a second free, shows up.

`Profile` already has a constructor, a destructor, a deep copy constructor and a `noexcept` move constructor. Give it assignment by **copy-and-swap**:

- a hidden friend `friend void swap(Profile& a, Profile& b) noexcept` that swaps the members;
- **one** assignment operator, `Profile& operator=(Profile other) noexcept`, taking its parameter **by value**, that swaps `*this` with `other` and returns `*this`.

That one operator then serves as both copy assignment and move assignment: `b = a` copies through the copy constructor, and `b = std::move(c)` moves through the move constructor, taking no block.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>
#include <utility>

// A stand-in for the heap that the checks can watch. Do not change it.
std::map<double*, std::size_t> g_live;
int g_allocs = 0;
int g_bad_frees = 0;
double* alloc_block(std::size_t n) {
    double* p = new double[n + 1]{};
    g_live[p] = n;
    ++g_allocs;
    return p;
}
void free_block(double* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    auto it = g_live.find(p);
    if (it == g_live.end()) { ++g_bad_frees; return; }
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < it->second; ++i) p[i] = -999.0;
    g_live.erase(it);
}

class Profile {
public:
    explicit Profile(std::size_t n) : n_(n), data_(alloc_block(n)) {}
    ~Profile() { free_block(data_); }
    Profile(const Profile& o) : n_(o.n_), data_(alloc_block(o.n_)) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n_; ++i) data_[i] = o.data_[i];
    }
    Profile(Profile&& o) noexcept : n_(std::exchange(o.n_, 0)), data_(std::exchange(o.data_, nullptr)) {}
    Profile& operator=(Profile other) noexcept { return *this; }
    std::size_t size() const { return n_; }
    double& operator[](std::size_t i) { return data_[i]; }
    const double* data() const { return data_; }
private:
    std::size_t n_;
    double* data_;
};
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>
#include <utility>

// A stand-in for the heap that the checks can watch. Do not change it.
std::map<double*, std::size_t> g_live;
int g_allocs = 0;
int g_bad_frees = 0;
double* alloc_block(std::size_t n) {
    double* p = new double[n + 1]{};
    g_live[p] = n;
    ++g_allocs;
    return p;
}
void free_block(double* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    auto it = g_live.find(p);
    if (it == g_live.end()) { ++g_bad_frees; return; }
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < it->second; ++i) p[i] = -999.0;
    g_live.erase(it);
}

class Profile {
public:
    explicit Profile(std::size_t n) : n_(n), data_(alloc_block(n)) {}
    ~Profile() { free_block(data_); }
    Profile(const Profile& o) : n_(o.n_), data_(alloc_block(o.n_)) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n_; ++i) data_[i] = o.data_[i];
    }
    Profile(Profile&& o) noexcept : n_(std::exchange(o.n_, 0)), data_(std::exchange(o.data_, nullptr)) {}

    // By value: the copy (or move) is made before the body runs. Then trade places;
    // other's destructor frees what this object held.
    Profile& operator=(Profile other) noexcept {
        swap(*this, other);
        return *this;
    }
    friend void swap(Profile& a, Profile& b) noexcept {
        std::swap(a.n_, b.n_);
        std::swap(a.data_, b.data_);
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return n_; }
    double& operator[](std::size_t i) { return data_[i]; }
    const double* data() const { return data_; }
private:
    std::size_t n_;
    double* data_;
};
--- hint
Swap the members, one at a time, with `std::swap(a.n_, b.n_)` and `std::swap(a.data_, b.data_)`. Never `std::swap` two whole `Profile`s inside your own assignment: that calls your assignment again.
--- hint
The body of the assignment is two lines: `swap(*this, other); return *this;`. The parameter's destructor does the cleaning up at the closing brace.
--- check test | Copy assignment copies and takes exactly one block
[] { Profile a(3); a[1] = 4.0; Profile b(7); int before = g_allocs; b = a; return g_allocs == before + 1 && b.size() == 3 && b[1] == 4.0 && b.data() != a.data(); }()
--- check test | Move assignment takes no block and keeps the source's block
[] { Profile c(5); const double* p = c.data(); Profile b(2); int before = g_allocs; b = std::move(c); return g_allocs == before && b.data() == p && b.size() == 5 && c.data() == nullptr; }()
--- check test | The old block is freed
[] { auto live = g_live.size(); Profile a(2); Profile b(9); b = a; b = Profile(4); return g_live.size() == live + 2 && g_bad_frees == 0; }()
--- check test | Self-assignment is safe
[] { Profile a(2); a[0] = 9.81; Profile& r = a; a = r; return a[0] == 9.81 && a.size() == 2 && g_bad_frees == 0; }()
--- check test | swap trades the blocks
[] { Profile a(1); Profile b(3); const double* pa = a.data(); const double* pb = b.data(); swap(a, b); return a.data() == pb && b.data() == pa && a.size() == 3; }()
--- check test | Nothing leaks
[] { auto live = g_live.size(); int bad = g_bad_frees; { Profile a(3); Profile b(1); b = a; a = std::move(b); b = a; } return g_live.size() == live && g_bad_frees == bad; }()
--- check source | One assignment operator, taking its parameter by value
operator=\s*\(\s*Profile\s+\w+\s*\)

+++ practice | A table shared by counting
--- task
The starter's heap stand-in, `alloc_block` and `free_block` with their counters, lets the checks watch every block: keep it exactly as it is. It never really frees a block: it fills a freed block with -999 and remembers it, so a read after a free, or a second free, shows up.

Several filters read one large lookup table, so copies should share it rather than duplicate it. Write `SharedTable`, which shares one block among its copies by **reference counting**, with all five special members:

- `SharedTable()` is empty; `SharedTable(std::size_t n, double fill)` makes a new table with one owner;
- a copy shares the table and adds one to its count; the destructor takes one away, and the last owner gives the block back with `free_block`;
- a move hands the table over without changing the count and leaves the source empty; both moves are `noexcept`;
- `long use_count() const` (0 when empty), `std::size_t size() const` (0 when empty), `double get(std::size_t i) const` and `void set(std::size_t i, double v)`, which every sharer sees.

The edge cases are what make this hard. `t = t` must change nothing. Assigning between two objects that **already share** the same table must not free it. Moving from an empty table must work.

Keep the table and its count together in a small heap-allocated `struct` that you create with `new` and delete with `delete`; only the samples come from `alloc_block`.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>

// A stand-in for the heap that the checks can watch. Do not change it.
std::map<double*, std::size_t> g_live;
int g_allocs = 0;
int g_bad_frees = 0;
double* alloc_block(std::size_t n) {
    double* p = new double[n + 1]{};
    g_live[p] = n;
    ++g_allocs;
    return p;
}
void free_block(double* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    auto it = g_live.find(p);
    if (it == g_live.end()) { ++g_bad_frees; return; }
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < it->second; ++i) p[i] = -999.0;
    g_live.erase(it);
}

class SharedTable {
public:
    SharedTable() {}
    SharedTable(std::size_t n, double fill) {}
    long use_count() const { return 0; }
    std::size_t size() const { return 0; }
    double get(std::size_t i) const { return 0.0; }
    void set(std::size_t i, double v) {}
};
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>
#include <utility>

// A stand-in for the heap that the checks can watch. Do not change it.
std::map<double*, std::size_t> g_live;
int g_allocs = 0;
int g_bad_frees = 0;
double* alloc_block(std::size_t n) {
    double* p = new double[n + 1]{};
    g_live[p] = n;
    ++g_allocs;
    return p;
}
void free_block(double* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    auto it = g_live.find(p);
    if (it == g_live.end()) { ++g_bad_frees; return; }
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < it->second; ++i) p[i] = -999.0;
    g_live.erase(it);
}

class SharedTable {
public:
    SharedTable() = default;
    SharedTable(std::size_t n, double fill) : c_(new Control{alloc_block(n), n, 1}) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < n; ++i) c_->data[i] = fill;
    }
    ~SharedTable() { release(); }

    SharedTable(const SharedTable& o) : c_(o.c_) {
        if (c_) ++c_->count;
    }
    SharedTable& operator=(const SharedTable& o) {
        // Take o's pointer and count the new owner first: if both already share one
        // table (or o is this object), the count goes up and back down, and the table
        // is never freed by mistake. Read o.c_ before release(), which clears c_.
        Control* incoming = o.c_;
        if (incoming) ++incoming->count;
        release();
        c_ = incoming;
        return *this;
    }
    SharedTable(SharedTable&& o) noexcept : c_(std::exchange(o.c_, nullptr)) {}
    SharedTable& operator=(SharedTable&& o) noexcept {
        if (this != &o) {
            release();
            c_ = std::exchange(o.c_, nullptr);
        }
        return *this;
    }

    long use_count() const { return c_ ? c_->count : 0; }
    std::size_t size() const { return c_ ? c_->n : 0; }
    double get(std::size_t i) const { return c_->data[i]; }
    void set(std::size_t i, double v) { c_->data[i] = v; }

private:
    struct Control {
        double* data;
        std::size_t n;
        long count;
    };
    void release() {
        if (c_ && --c_->count == 0) {
            free_block(c_->data);
            delete c_;
        }
        c_ = nullptr;
    }
    Control* c_ = nullptr;
};
--- hint
Hold one pointer to a small struct with three fields: the block, its size, and how many `SharedTable`s point at it. A private `release()` takes one owner away and frees both when the count reaches 0.
--- hint
In copy assignment, save the source's pointer and add one to its count **before** you release your own. Then `t = t`, and assignment between two sharers, both come out right with no special case.
--- hint
A move changes no count at all: the new object takes the pointer, and the old one lets go of it.
--- check test | Copies share one table
[] { SharedTable a(4, 1.0); SharedTable b = a; b.set(2, 7.0); return a.get(2) == 7.0 && a.use_count() == 2 && b.use_count() == 2; }()
--- check test | The block is freed when the last owner goes
[] { auto live = g_live.size(); SharedTable* keep = nullptr; { SharedTable a(3, 0.0); SharedTable b = a; keep = new SharedTable(b); } bool alive = g_live.size() == live + 1 && keep->use_count() == 1; delete keep; return alive && g_live.size() == live && g_bad_frees == 0; }()
--- check test | Self-assignment changes nothing
[] { SharedTable a(2, 5.0); SharedTable& r = a; a = r; return a.use_count() == 1 && a.get(1) == 5.0 && g_bad_frees == 0; }()
--- check test | Assigning between two sharers keeps the table
[] { SharedTable a(2, 3.0); SharedTable b = a; a = b; b = a; return a.use_count() == 2 && a.get(0) == 3.0 && g_bad_frees == 0; }()
--- check test | Assignment lets go of the old table
[] { auto live = g_live.size(); SharedTable a(2, 1.0); SharedTable b(9, 0.0); b = a; return g_live.size() == live + 1 && a.use_count() == 2 && b.size() == 2; }()
--- check test | A move hands over without changing the count
[] { SharedTable a(3, 1.0); SharedTable b = a; SharedTable c = std::move(a); return c.use_count() == 2 && a.use_count() == 0 && a.size() == 0; }()
--- check test | Moving an empty table, and move assignment between sharers
[] { SharedTable e; SharedTable f = std::move(e); SharedTable a(1, 2.0); SharedTable b = a; a = std::move(b); return f.use_count() == 0 && a.use_count() == 1 && b.use_count() == 0 && a.get(0) == 2.0 && g_bad_frees == 0; }()
--- check test | Moves are noexcept
std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<SharedTable> && std::is_nothrow_move_assignable_v<SharedTable>

+++ practice | A slot copied onto itself
--- task
The starter's heap stand-in, `alloc_block` and `free_block` with their counters, lets the checks watch every block: keep it exactly as it is. It never really frees a block: it fills a freed block with -999 and remembers it, so a read after a free, or a second free, shows up.

`Attitude` holds a 3×3 direction cosine matrix in a heap block, and starts as the identity. `copy_slot(slots, from, to)` copies one saved attitude over another. It works for two different slots, but when `from` and `to` are the same slot, that slot comes out all zeros instead of unchanged.

Find the bug in `Attitude` and fix it. Keep `copy_slot` as it is.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>
#include <vector>

// A stand-in for the heap that the checks can watch. Do not change it.
std::map<double*, std::size_t> g_live;
int g_allocs = 0;
int g_bad_frees = 0;
double* alloc_block(std::size_t n) {
    double* p = new double[n + 1]{};
    g_live[p] = n;
    ++g_allocs;
    return p;
}
void free_block(double* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    auto it = g_live.find(p);
    if (it == g_live.end()) { ++g_bad_frees; return; }
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < it->second; ++i) p[i] = -999.0;
    g_live.erase(it);
}

class Attitude {
public:
    Attitude() : m_(alloc_block(9)) { m_[0] = m_[4] = m_[8] = 1.0; }
    ~Attitude() { free_block(m_); }
    Attitude(const Attitude& o) : m_(alloc_block(9)) {
        for (int i = 0; i < 9; ++i) m_[i] = o.m_[i];
    }
    Attitude& operator=(const Attitude& o) {
        free_block(m_);
        m_ = alloc_block(9);
        for (int i = 0; i < 9; ++i) m_[i] = o.m_[i];
        return *this;
    }
    double& at(int r, int c) { return m_[3 * r + c]; }
    double at(int r, int c) const { return m_[3 * r + c]; }
private:
    double* m_;
};

void copy_slot(std::vector<Attitude>& slots, std::size_t from, std::size_t to) {
    slots[to] = slots[from];
}
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>
#include <vector>

// A stand-in for the heap that the checks can watch. Do not change it.
std::map<double*, std::size_t> g_live;
int g_allocs = 0;
int g_bad_frees = 0;
double* alloc_block(std::size_t n) {
    double* p = new double[n + 1]{};
    g_live[p] = n;
    ++g_allocs;
    return p;
}
void free_block(double* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    auto it = g_live.find(p);
    if (it == g_live.end()) { ++g_bad_frees; return; }
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < it->second; ++i) p[i] = -999.0;
    g_live.erase(it);
}

class Attitude {
public:
    Attitude() : m_(alloc_block(9)) { m_[0] = m_[4] = m_[8] = 1.0; }
    ~Attitude() { free_block(m_); }
    Attitude(const Attitude& o) : m_(alloc_block(9)) {
        for (int i = 0; i < 9; ++i) m_[i] = o.m_[i];
    }
    // Build the new block from o first, and free the old one last:
    // when o is this object, the values are copied before anything is freed.
    Attitude& operator=(const Attitude& o) {
        double* fresh = alloc_block(9);
        for (int i = 0; i < 9; ++i) fresh[i] = o.m_[i];
        free_block(m_);
        m_ = fresh;
        return *this;
    }
    double& at(int r, int c) { return m_[3 * r + c]; }
    double at(int r, int c) const { return m_[3 * r + c]; }
private:
    double* m_;
};

void copy_slot(std::vector<Attitude>& slots, std::size_t from, std::size_t to) {
    slots[to] = slots[from];
}
--- hint
When `from` equals `to`, `o` and `*this` are the same object. Follow `o.m_` through the three lines of the assignment.
--- hint
After `m_ = alloc_block(9);`, `o.m_` is that new, zeroed block too, so the loop copies zeros onto zeros. Copy from `o` before you free or replace anything.
--- check test | Copying a slot onto itself keeps it
[] { std::vector<Attitude> s(3); s[2].at(0, 1) = 0.5; copy_slot(s, 2, 2); return s[2].at(0, 0) == 1.0 && s[2].at(0, 1) == 0.5 && s[2].at(2, 2) == 1.0; }()
--- check test | Copying between two slots
[] { std::vector<Attitude> s(2); s[0].at(1, 2) = -0.25; copy_slot(s, 0, 1); s[0].at(1, 2) = 9.0; return s[1].at(1, 2) == -0.25 && s[1].at(1, 1) == 1.0; }()
--- check test | No bad frees and no leaks
[] { auto live = g_live.size(); int bad = g_bad_frees; { std::vector<Attitude> s(3); copy_slot(s, 0, 1); copy_slot(s, 1, 1); copy_slot(s, 2, 0); } return g_live.size() == live && g_bad_frees == bad; }()

+++ practice | A series that grows
--- task
The starter's heap stand-in, `alloc_block` and `free_block` with their counters, lets the checks watch every block: keep it exactly as it is. It never really frees a block: it fills a freed block with -999 and remembers it, so a read after a free, or a second free, shows up.

Write `Series`, a growable array of doubles that owns its storage from `alloc_block`, by the rule of five with copy-and-swap:

- `Series()` is empty, with capacity 0 and no block;
- `void push_back(double x)`: when the series is full, it grows the capacity from 0 to 1, then doubles it (1, 2, 4, 8, and so on). Growing takes a new block, copies the values, and frees the old block;
- the copy constructor takes a block of exactly `size()` elements, so the copy's capacity equals its size, and copying an empty series takes no block;
- the move constructor is `noexcept`, takes no block, and leaves the source empty with capacity 0;
- `friend void swap(Series& a, Series& b) noexcept`, and one assignment operator `Series& operator=(Series other) noexcept`, by copy-and-swap;
- `std::size_t size() const`, `std::size_t capacity() const`, `double& operator[](std::size_t i)` and `double operator[](std::size_t i) const`.

So pushing 10 values into an empty series takes 5 blocks, for capacities 1, 2, 4, 8 and 16.
--- starter
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>
#include <utility>

// A stand-in for the heap that the checks can watch. Do not change it.
std::map<double*, std::size_t> g_live;
int g_allocs = 0;
int g_bad_frees = 0;
double* alloc_block(std::size_t n) {
    double* p = new double[n + 1]{};
    g_live[p] = n;
    ++g_allocs;
    return p;
}
void free_block(double* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    auto it = g_live.find(p);
    if (it == g_live.end()) { ++g_bad_frees; return; }
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < it->second; ++i) p[i] = -999.0;
    g_live.erase(it);
}

class Series {
public:
    Series() {}
    void push_back(double x) {}
    std::size_t size() const { return 0; }
    std::size_t capacity() const { return 0; }
    double operator[](std::size_t i) const { return 0.0; }
};
--- solution
#include <cstddef>
#include <map>
#include <utility>

// A stand-in for the heap that the checks can watch. Do not change it.
std::map<double*, std::size_t> g_live;
int g_allocs = 0;
int g_bad_frees = 0;
double* alloc_block(std::size_t n) {
    double* p = new double[n + 1]{};
    g_live[p] = n;
    ++g_allocs;
    return p;
}
void free_block(double* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) return;
    auto it = g_live.find(p);
    if (it == g_live.end()) { ++g_bad_frees; return; }
    for (std::size_t i = 0; i < it->second; ++i) p[i] = -999.0;
    g_live.erase(it);
}

class Series {
public:
    Series() = default;
    ~Series() { free_block(data_); }

    Series(const Series& o) : size_(o.size_), cap_(o.size_), data_(o.size_ ? alloc_block(o.size_) : nullptr) {
        for (std::size_t i = 0; i < size_; ++i) data_[i] = o.data_[i];
    }
    Series(Series&& o) noexcept
        : size_(std::exchange(o.size_, 0)), cap_(std::exchange(o.cap_, 0)), data_(std::exchange(o.data_, nullptr)) {}

    // Copy-and-swap: one operator for copy and move assignment.
    Series& operator=(Series other) noexcept {
        swap(*this, other);
        return *this;
    }
    friend void swap(Series& a, Series& b) noexcept {
        std::swap(a.size_, b.size_);
        std::swap(a.cap_, b.cap_);
        std::swap(a.data_, b.data_);
    }

    void push_back(double x) {
        if (size_ == cap_) {
            const std::size_t new_cap = cap_ == 0 ? 1 : 2 * cap_;
            double* fresh = alloc_block(new_cap);        // new block first
            for (std::size_t i = 0; i < size_; ++i) fresh[i] = data_[i];
            free_block(data_);                           // then let the old one go
            data_ = fresh;
            cap_ = new_cap;
        }
        data_[size_++] = x;
    }

    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    std::size_t capacity() const { return cap_; }
    double& operator[](std::size_t i) { return data_[i]; }
    double operator[](std::size_t i) const { return data_[i]; }

private:
    std::size_t size_ = 0;
    std::size_t cap_ = 0;
    double* data_ = nullptr;
};
--- hint
Three members: the size, the capacity and the pointer. Default member initializers of 0, 0 and `nullptr` make the default constructor trivial.
--- hint
`push_back` grows only when `size_ == cap_`. The copy constructor sizes its block from the source's size, not its capacity.
--- hint
Swap all three members in `swap`, and write the assignment as `swap(*this, other); return *this;`.
--- check test | Ten pushes take five blocks
[] { int before = g_allocs; Series s; for (int i = 0; i < 10; ++i) s.push_back(i * 1.0); return g_allocs == before + 5 && s.capacity() == 16 && s.size() == 10 && s[9] == 9.0; }()
--- check test | Growth frees the old blocks
[] { auto live = g_live.size(); Series s; for (int i = 0; i < 20; ++i) s.push_back(1.0); return g_live.size() == live + 1 && g_bad_frees == 0; }()
--- check test | A copy is exactly as big as it needs to be
[] { Series a; for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) a.push_back(i * 2.0); int before = g_allocs; Series b = a; return g_allocs == before + 1 && b.capacity() == 5 && b.size() == 5 && b[4] == 8.0; }()
--- check test | Copying an empty series takes no block
[] { Series e; int before = g_allocs; Series c = e; return g_allocs == before && c.capacity() == 0; }()
--- check test | A move takes no block and empties the source
[] { Series a; a.push_back(1.0); a.push_back(2.0); int before = g_allocs; Series b = std::move(a); return g_allocs == before && b.size() == 2 && a.size() == 0 && a.capacity() == 0; }()
--- check test | Assignment by copy and by move
[] { Series a; a.push_back(3.0); Series b; b.push_back(1.0); b.push_back(1.0); int before = g_allocs; b = a; bool copied = g_allocs == before + 1 && b.size() == 1 && b[0] == 3.0; Series c; c.push_back(4.0); before = g_allocs; b = std::move(c); return copied && g_allocs == before && b[0] == 4.0; }()
--- check test | A copy is independent, and a moved-from series can grow again
[] { Series a; a.push_back(1.0); Series b = a; b[0] = 5.0; Series c = std::move(a); a.push_back(7.0); return c[0] == 1.0 && b[0] == 5.0 && a.size() == 1 && a[0] == 7.0; }()
--- check test | Nothing leaks, and moves are noexcept
[] { auto live = g_live.size(); int bad = g_bad_frees; { Series a; for (int i = 0; i < 9; ++i) a.push_back(1.0); Series b = a; a = b; b = std::move(a); Series& r = b; b = r; } return g_live.size() == live && g_bad_frees == bad && std::is_nothrow_move_constructible_v<Series>; }()

+++ question | Why the by-value assignment can be noexcept
--- ask
In copy-and-swap, the assignment operator is often declared

```cpp
Profile& operator=(Profile other) noexcept;
```

even though copying a `Profile` allocates memory, which can throw. Why is that `noexcept` honest?
--- choice
Because the compiler turns the copy into a move whenever the operator is `noexcept`.
--- choice correct
Because the copy that can throw is made while the argument is passed, before the function is entered; inside the function there is only a swap and a destructor, and neither throws.
--- choice
Because `noexcept` on an assignment operator is ignored by the compiler.
--- choice
It is not honest: if the copy throws, `std::terminate` is called, which is the intended behavior.
--- why
A by-value parameter is initialized by the caller, as part of the call, before the function body starts. If that copy throws, the exception leaves the call site and the assignment operator never runs, so its `noexcept` promise is untouched. The body itself only swaps pointers and, at the closing brace, destroys `other`, and both of those cannot fail. That is also why the target is untouched if the copy fails: the strong guarantee.

=== cpp03-l08 | Practice: const member functions, static members and friends
--- for
l08-const-static-and-friend

+++ practice | A quaternion the logger can read
--- task
The attitude logger only ever has a `const Quaternion&`, and it cannot call anything on it: none of the starter's member functions is marked `const`, although most of them only read.

Make `Quaternion` const-correct. Mark `const` every member function that does not change the quaternion, and only those; `normalize()` changes it, so it must stay non-const. Then write two free functions that take `const Quaternion&`:

- `double dot(const Quaternion& a, const Quaternion& b)`, the sum of the products of the four components;
- `double angle_between(const Quaternion& a, const Quaternion& b)`, the rotation angle in radians between two attitudes: 2 × acos(|dot(a', b')|), where a' and b' are the normalized quaternions. Clamp the absolute dot product to at most 1 before `acos`, because rounding can push it just above.
--- starter
#include <cmath>

class Quaternion {
public:
    Quaternion(double w, double x, double y, double z) : w_(w), x_(x), y_(y), z_(z) {}
    double w() { return w_; }
    double x() { return x_; }
    double y() { return y_; }
    double z() { return z_; }
    double norm() { return std::sqrt(w_ * w_ + x_ * x_ + y_ * y_ + z_ * z_); }
    // A unit-length copy. A zero quaternion gives the identity (1, 0, 0, 0).
    Quaternion normalized() {
        const double n = norm();
        if (n == 0.0) return Quaternion(1.0, 0.0, 0.0, 0.0);
        return Quaternion(w_ / n, x_ / n, y_ / n, z_ / n);
    }
    // The same, in place.
    void normalize() { *this = normalized(); }
    Quaternion conjugate() { return Quaternion(w_, -x_, -y_, -z_); }
private:
    double w_, x_, y_, z_;
};
--- solution
#include <cmath>

class Quaternion {
public:
    Quaternion(double w, double x, double y, double z) : w_(w), x_(x), y_(y), z_(z) {}
    double w() const { return w_; }
    double x() const { return x_; }
    double y() const { return y_; }
    double z() const { return z_; }
    double norm() const { return std::sqrt(w_ * w_ + x_ * x_ + y_ * y_ + z_ * z_); }
    // A unit-length copy. A zero quaternion gives the identity (1, 0, 0, 0).
    Quaternion normalized() const {
        const double n = norm();
        if (n == 0.0) return Quaternion(1.0, 0.0, 0.0, 0.0);
        return Quaternion(w_ / n, x_ / n, y_ / n, z_ / n);
    }
    // The same, in place: it changes the object, so it is not const.
    void normalize() { *this = normalized(); }
    Quaternion conjugate() const { return Quaternion(w_, -x_, -y_, -z_); }
private:
    double w_, x_, y_, z_;
};

double dot(const Quaternion& a, const Quaternion& b) {
    return a.w() * b.w() + a.x() * b.x() + a.y() * b.y() + a.z() * b.z();
}

double angle_between(const Quaternion& a, const Quaternion& b) {
    double d = std::fabs(dot(a.normalized(), b.normalized()));
    if (d > 1.0) d = 1.0;
    return 2.0 * std::acos(d);
}
--- hint
A `const` goes after the parameter list: `double norm() const`. On a `const` object, only such functions can be called.
--- hint
`normalized()` returns a new quaternion and leaves this one alone, so it can be `const`. `normalize()` assigns to `*this`, so it cannot.
--- check test | Reading a const quaternion
[] { const Quaternion q(0.0, 3.0, 0.0, 4.0); return q.norm() == 5.0 && q.x() == 3.0 && q.conjugate().z() == -4.0; }()
--- check test | normalized works on a const quaternion and leaves it alone
[] { const Quaternion q(0.0, 3.0, 0.0, 4.0); const Quaternion u = q.normalized(); return std::fabs(u.norm() - 1.0) < 1e-15 && std::fabs(u.x() - 0.6) < 1e-15 && q.norm() == 5.0; }()
--- check test | A zero quaternion normalizes to the identity
[] { Quaternion q(0.0, 0.0, 0.0, 0.0); q.normalize(); return q.w() == 1.0 && q.x() == 0.0; }()
--- check test | dot of two const quaternions
[] { const Quaternion a(1.0, 2.0, 3.0, 4.0); const Quaternion b(0.5, -1.0, 0.0, 2.0); return dot(a, b) == 6.5; }()
--- check test | 90 degrees about z
[] { const double h = std::sqrt(0.5); const Quaternion a(1.0, 0.0, 0.0, 0.0); const Quaternion b(h, 0.0, 0.0, h); return std::fabs(angle_between(a, b) - 1.5707963267948966) < 1e-9; }()
--- check test | The same attitude, even unnormalized or negated, is 0 apart
[] { const Quaternion a(2.0, 0.0, 0.0, 0.0); const Quaternion b(-1.0, 0.0, 0.0, 0.0); return angle_between(a, b) == 0.0; }()

+++ practice | A path length worked out once
--- task
A ground track is a list of points, and its length is the sum of the straight segments between them. Working it out walks every point, so `Polyline` caches it: `length()` computes the length only when the cache is stale, and `add()` makes it stale.

Write `Polyline`:

- `void add(double x, double y)` appends a point and marks the cache stale;
- `double length() const`: returns the cached length, working it out first if it is stale. 0.0 for fewer than two points;
- `int computations() const`: how many times the length has actually been worked out.

`length()` must be callable on a `const Polyline&`, so use `mutable` for the cache and the counter, and for nothing else.
--- starter
#include <cmath>
#include <vector>

class Polyline {
public:
    void add(double x, double y) {}
    double length() { return 0.0; }
    int computations() { return 0; }
};
--- solution
#include <cmath>
#include <vector>

class Polyline {
public:
    void add(double x, double y) {
        xs_.push_back(x);
        ys_.push_back(y);
        valid_ = false;                  // the cached length is stale now
    }
    double length() const {
        if (!valid_) {
            double sum = 0.0;
            for (std::size_t i = 1; i < xs_.size(); ++i) sum += std::hypot(xs_[i] - xs_[i - 1], ys_[i] - ys_[i - 1]);
            cache_ = sum;                // allowed in a const function: the cache is mutable
            valid_ = true;
            ++computations_;
        }
        return cache_;
    }
    int computations() const { return computations_; }

private:
    std::vector<double> xs_, ys_;        // the observable state: not mutable
    mutable double cache_ = 0.0;
    mutable bool valid_ = false;
    mutable int computations_ = 0;
};
--- hint
The points are the observable state, so they are not `mutable`. The cached length, its "valid" flag and the counter are private bookkeeping that no caller can see change the answer.
--- hint
`std::hypot(dx, dy)` is the length of one segment. Start the loop at the second point.
--- check test | A two-segment track through a const reference
[] { Polyline p; p.add(0.0, 0.0); p.add(3.0, 4.0); p.add(3.0, 10.0); const Polyline& r = p; return r.length() == 11.0; }()
--- check test | Asking twice works it out once
[] { Polyline p; p.add(0.0, 0.0); p.add(1.0, 0.0); const Polyline& r = p; r.length(); r.length(); r.length(); return r.computations() == 1; }()
--- check test | Adding a point makes it work it out again
[] { Polyline p; p.add(0.0, 0.0); p.add(1.0, 0.0); p.length(); p.add(1.0, 2.0); double l = p.length(); return l == 3.0 && p.computations() == 2; }()
--- check test | Fewer than two points have length 0
[] { Polyline e; Polyline one; one.add(5.0, 5.0); return e.length() == 0.0 && one.length() == 0.0; }()

+++ practice | A track counter that counts copies
--- task
A radar tracker wants to know how many tracks are alive and how many were ever made. Write `Track` with a static count of each:

- `explicit Track(std::string name)`: a new track;
- a copy constructor: a copy is a **new** track too, so it counts and gets a new serial, with the same name;
- a copy assignment: it copies the name only. No new track is made, and the serial stays;
- a destructor;
- `static int live()`: tracks alive now; `static int created()`: tracks ever made, copies included;
- `int serial() const`: 1 for the first track ever made, 2 for the next, and so on, copies included;
- `const std::string& name() const`.

Moves are not declared, so a move is a copy here and counts like one.
--- starter
#include <string>

class Track {
public:
    explicit Track(std::string name) : name_(name) {}
    static int live() { return 0; }
    static int created() { return 0; }
    int serial() const { return 0; }
    const std::string& name() const { return name_; }
private:
    std::string name_;
};
--- solution
#include <string>

class Track {
public:
    explicit Track(std::string name) : name_(name), serial_(++created_) { ++live_; }
    Track(const Track& o) : name_(o.name_), serial_(++created_) { ++live_; }   // every constructor counts
    Track& operator=(const Track& o) {                                            // no new object: no count
        name_ = o.name_;
        return *this;
    }
    ~Track() { --live_; }

    static int live() { return live_; }
    static int created() { return created_; }
    int serial() const { return serial_; }
    const std::string& name() const { return name_; }

private:
    std::string name_;
    int serial_;
    inline static int live_ = 0;       // one of each for the whole class
    inline static int created_ = 0;
};
--- hint
`inline static int live_ = 0;` inside the class is one counter shared by every `Track`. Every constructor adds one to it, and the destructor takes one away.
--- hint
The serial comes from the "ever made" counter: `serial_(++created_)` in both constructors.
--- check test | Two tracks and a copy
[] { int l0 = Track::live(), c0 = Track::created(); Track a("alpha"); Track b("bravo"); Track c = a; return Track::live() == l0 + 3 && Track::created() == c0 + 3 && c.name() == "alpha"; }()
--- check test | A copy gets the next serial
[] { Track a("x"); Track b = a; return b.serial() == a.serial() + 1; }()
--- check test | Assignment makes no new track and keeps the serial
[] { Track a("a"); Track b("b"); int s = b.serial(); int c0 = Track::created(); b = a; return b.name() == "a" && b.serial() == s && Track::created() == c0; }()
--- check test | Everything counted is uncounted at the end
[] { int l0 = Track::live(); { Track a("a"); std::vector<Track> v; v.push_back(a); v.push_back(a); v.push_back(Track("t")); } return Track::live() == l0; }()
--- check test | Counts are read through the class, with no object
[] { int c0 = Track::created(); { Track t("z"); } return Track::created() == c0 + 1; }()

+++ practice | Temperatures that cannot be impossible
--- task
Nothing is colder than 0 kelvin. Write `Temperature` so that the only way to make one is through three static named constructors, which check it:

- `static Temperature from_kelvin(double k)`;
- `static Temperature from_celsius(double c)`, where kelvin = c + 273.15;
- `static Temperature from_fahrenheit(double f)`, where kelvin = (f + 459.67) × 5 / 9.

If the kelvin value is below 0, or is not a number (NaN), the temperature is invalid and holds 0 kelvin. Exactly 0 is valid. Give the class a **private** constructor, so outside code cannot build one any other way, and no default constructor. Also write `double kelvin() const`, `double celsius() const` and `bool valid() const`.
--- starter
class Temperature {
public:
    Temperature(double k) : k_(k) {}
    double kelvin() const { return k_; }
    double celsius() const { return 0.0; }
    bool valid() const { return true; }
private:
    double k_;
};
--- solution
class Temperature {
public:
    static Temperature from_kelvin(double k) {
        const bool ok = k >= 0.0;              // false for NaN too: every comparison with NaN is false
        return Temperature(ok ? k : 0.0, ok);
    }
